  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I’m here because this has been going on for months at work, and it’s gotten to the point where I can’t just keep trying to handle it myself. I work as an interpreter and translator, mostly with project files and translation memory, and I keep seeing the same pattern over and over.\n\nCertain subcontractors—and honestly, one colleague in particular—keep making these tiny changes. Not big obvious edits, but little things in the translation memory, little timestamp shifts, small adjustments that later make it look like my quality control failed. And then when something is questioned, it’s all positioned so it points back to me, like I missed something or mishandled the file.\n\nI’ve asked IT to look at it, and they say the audit trail is clean, but that doesn’t reassure me because the record itself has been arranged. That’s the whole point—it’s clean because it’s been made to look clean. I know how these files work, and I know what normal errors look like. This isn’t random. It’s too consistent. It feels like they’re setting it up so when there’s a problem, I’m the one who gets blamed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that there could be another explanation for these changes that doesn't involve someone intentionally trying to sabotage you?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to consider that, yes. I mean, I’m not saying every single typo or every timestamp discrepancy in a project file is sabotage. I know how messy translation workflows can get—people update segments, memories get merged, subcontractors work in different time zones, files get passed around, and sometimes the software does odd things. I do understand that.\n\nBut this is different. It’s been months, and it’s the same kind of pattern: small changes in the translation memory, little shifts in timestamps, things that don’t look dramatic by themselves but later make it appear as if my quality control missed something. And it’s not spread randomly across everyone’s work. It keeps circling back to work I touched, or files where I’m responsible for final checks.\n\nAnd the colleague I mentioned—there are just too many coincidences there. The changes happen in a way that is subtle enough that, if I raise it, I sound like I’m overreacting. Then IT says the audit trail is clean, but that doesn’t settle it for me, because if someone knows what they’re doing, the record can be arranged to look clean. That’s what worries me. It’s almost designed so there’s no obvious smoking gun, just enough to make me look careless.\n\nSo yes, technically there could be another explanation for one or two of the changes. But taken together? No, I don’t think it’s innocent. I think someone is setting it up so the blame lands on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anyone else shares your concern about being sabotaged at work?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really in the way I mean it. Other people will admit there are “oddities” in the files or that a timestamp looks strange, or they’ll say something like, “Yes, that segment was different before,” but then they back away from saying what it actually suggests.\n\nThat’s part of what makes it so frustrating. I’m the one seeing the whole pattern because I’m the one doing the final quality control, so I see how these tiny translation memory changes and timestamp shifts accumulate. A subcontractor changes something here, then that same kind of issue appears again later, and then somehow it reflects on my work. And with this one colleague, it keeps happening around files connected to me. It’s not like one dramatic incident where everyone would say, “Yes, that’s sabotage.” It’s small enough that each piece can be dismissed.\n\nIT doesn’t share my concern, at least not officially. They keep saying the audit trail is clean, but that doesn’t reassure me, because if the record has been arranged properly, of course it looks clean. That’s exactly the mechanism. So, no, I don’t think anyone is openly saying, “Yes, you’re being set up.” But I think some people notice things and either don’t want to get involved or don’t understand enough about the workflow to see how it all points back to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant stress or anxiety in your personal life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—not in the sense of some big personal crisis outside of work. I mean, life is life, obviously. There are bills, family routines, errands, things around the house, the normal pressures that everyone has. But I’m still doing those things. I still translate, I still manage the team’s workflow, I pay what needs to be paid, I keep up with my family responsibilities.\n\nThat’s part of why I keep coming back to the work files, because outside of that issue I’m functioning pretty normally. I’m not falling apart across the board. It’s when the file history comes up, or when certain coworkers are involved, especially around those project records and translation memory changes, that things really start to go off track for me. That’s where I get stuck, because it feels like the problem is there, not just some general stress from my personal life spilling over into everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible that you might be mistaken about someone sabotaging you at work?\n\nPatient: I suppose in the abstract, yes, anyone can be mistaken. I understand that. I’m not saying I’m incapable of misreading something, or that every single odd file entry automatically means someone is out to get me.\n\nBut with this, no… I don’t really think I’m mistaken. That’s why I’m here, because for months I’ve been seeing the same pattern in the project files. It’s not one strange timestamp or one segment in the translation memory being different. It’s certain subcontractors, and especially one colleague, connected again and again to these tiny changes that later make it look like my quality control failed.\n\nAnd the changes are always small enough that if I point them out individually, people can say, “Well, that could be a software issue,” or, “Maybe someone updated it during the normal workflow.” But when you’re the one responsible for the final checks, and you keep seeing these little shifts lining up in a way that puts the blame on you, it stops feeling like coincidence.\n\nIT keeps saying the audit trail is clean, but that doesn’t reassure me. If someone knows the system well enough, they can arrange the record so it looks clean. That’s exactly what makes it hard to prove. So yes, I can say it’s possible in a philosophical sense that I’m mistaken, but honestly, I don’t believe that’s what’s happening. I think the record has been arranged to make me look careless.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe your working relationship with this colleague before you started noticing these patterns in the project files?\n\nPatient: Before all of this, I would have said it was… normal. Professional. Not especially close, but not hostile either, at least not that I recognized at the time.\n\nWe worked on overlapping projects, so there was the usual back-and-forth about terminology, deadlines, version control, that sort of thing. They may say it was just an oversight, but it’s happened enough that I’ve noticed.\n\nWith friends, less so. I’m not sure I’d say I act very differently with them, except maybe I’m more preoccupied if work comes up. But at work, yes, the atmosphere has changed. I can feel people trying to avoid getting into it with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep since you started noticing these issues at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d call it a sleep problem, exactly. I mean, I sleep. I haven’t been tracking it with an app or anything, so I couldn’t give you a neat number of hours or say, “This changed on this date.” Some nights I might lie there thinking about work longer than I’d like, but I think a lot of people do that when there’s tension at the office.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m up all night or unable to function the next day. It’s more that if something happened that day with the files or someone dismissed what I was pointing out, then yes, it can take me longer to settle down because my mind keeps going back over the sequence—who changed what, when the email came in, whether I answered it clearly enough. But on nights when nothing work-related is pressing, I don’t especially notice my sleep being different.\n\nSo… not a major issue, I guess. Not compared with the work situation itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility, no matter how small, that you might be misinterpreting the situation at work?\n\nPatient: I’ve tried to consider that, honestly. I know it probably sounds like I’m being rigid, and I can understand why someone outside the work might say, “Couldn’t this just be normal edits or system quirks?” I’ve asked myself that more than once.\n\nBut the problem is, it’s not one isolated thing. It’s been months of the same kind of pattern in the project files. Certain subcontractors’ names come up, and one colleague in particular seems to be connected to these very small changes—tiny adjustments in the translation memory, timestamps that don’t look right in context, little shifts that by themselves can be explained away, but together make it look as if my quality control missed something.\n\nAnd that’s what people don’t seem to understand. If one timestamp is odd, fine, maybe I misread it. If one file has a strange update, fine, maybe someone corrected something and forgot to mention it. But when it keeps happening in ways that put the responsibility back on me, then at a certain point it stops feeling like a misunderstanding.\n\nIT has said the audit trail is clean, yes. I know that. But “clean” just means the system doesn’t show obvious tampering. It doesn’t mean the record hasn’t been arranged in a way that points blame toward me. So… could I be misinterpreting one or two details? Maybe. I’m not saying I’m incapable of making a mistake. But the overall situation? No, I don’t think I’m imagining that. The pattern is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your colleague is intentionally trying to sabotage you for a specific reason?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can tell you the exact reason, like some dramatic motive. I’m not saying I have a confession from him or an email where he says, “I’m going to sabotage you.” It isn’t like that.\n\nBut yes, I do think it’s intentional. That’s the part I keep coming back to. For months now, it’s been the same kind of thing in the project files—small changes, not big obvious ones. Certain subcontractors’ names keep showing up, and this one colleague seems to be connected around the times these little adjustments happen in the translation memory or the timestamps. And then later, when the issue is looked at, it creates the impression that my quality control missed something or that I failed to catch an inconsistency.\n\nIf it happened once, I’d probably accept that it was just a normal correction or a system issue. But it’s the repetition. It’s the way the record ends up pointing back to me. That doesn’t feel accidental.\n\nAs for why, I can only speculate. Maybe he wants to make himself look more reliable by making me look careless. Maybe there’s some internal competition I wasn’t fully aware of. Maybe he just doesn’t like that I’m careful and I notice things. I don’t know. But I don’t think these changes are innocent. Even when IT says the audit trail is clean, that doesn’t reassure me much, because a clean trail doesn’t mean the record hasn’t been arranged in a way that puts the blame on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could happen, or any evidence that could come up, that would make you change your mind about someone sabotaging you at work?\n\nPatient: I suppose, in theory, yes. I mean, I’m not trying to say I’m incapable of changing my mind if there were something genuinely clear. If someone could show me, step by step, that these changes in the project files were normal and that the same subcontractors showing up, and this colleague being around those tiny edits to the translation memory and timestamps, all of that had an innocent explanation that actually fit the sequence… I would have to look at that.\n\nBut that’s the issue: so far, what I’ve been given doesn’t explain the pattern. IT saying the audit trail is clean doesn’t settle it for me, because “clean” just means the system isn’t showing obvious tampering. It doesn’t mean the record hasn’t been arranged in a way that makes it look like my quality control failed. That’s the part that keeps getting brushed aside.\n\nSo if there were some evidence that accounted for the whole chain of things—not just one file, not just one timestamp, but months of these small changes that keep pointing back toward me—then yes, I’d listen. But if it’s just someone saying, “No, everything looks fine,” or “You’re reading too much into it,” then no, that wouldn’t change my mind. Because I’ve seen the same pattern too many times.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's possible for you to be wrong about someone sabotaging you at work, or do you believe it's a fact?\n\nPatient: I understand why you’re asking it that way. I can say, in some abstract sense, yes, a person can be wrong. I know that. I’m not saying I’m immune to mistakes or that every tiny thing I’ve noticed has to mean something by itself.\n\nBut if you’re asking me whether I believe I’m wrong about this situation at work, then no, I don’t think I am. At this point it feels like a fact, because it isn’t just one misunderstanding or one file that I got anxious about. For months I’ve been seeing the same pattern in the project files. The same kinds of tiny changes show up in the translation memory and timestamps, certain subcontractors’ names keep appearing around those changes, and one colleague in particular keeps seeming connected to it. Then later the record makes it look like my quality control failed, like I missed something I should have caught.\n\nThat’s the part that makes it hard for me to treat it as just a possibility. If it were random, I would expect it to look random. But it doesn’t. It keeps arranging itself in a way that puts the blame on me.\n\nAnd yes, IT says the audit trail is clean. I’ve heard that. But to me that doesn’t disprove it. A clean audit trail just means there’s nothing obvious showing as tampering. It doesn’t mean the record hasn’t been set up carefully enough to look legitimate while still pointing back at me.\n\nSo… could I be wrong in theory? I suppose. But based on what I’ve seen, I believe someone has been arranging those records to make me look responsible. I don’t really see it as just a suspicion anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief about being sabotaged at work might be an exaggeration or distortion of reality?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s an exaggeration, no. I understand that from the outside it might sound like I’m connecting things too tightly, or like I’m putting too much meaning into small edits. I do understand how it could look that way if someone hasn’t been living with it day after day.\n\nBut I’m here because this has been going on for months, and it’s the same pattern in the project files. It isn’t one odd timestamp or one subcontractor name showing up once. It’s these tiny changes in the translation memory, little timestamp shifts, certain subcontractors appearing around them, and the same colleague seeming to be involved or nearby in the sequence. Then somehow, later, the way the record reads makes it look like my quality control failed. Like I missed something obvious. Like I’m the careless one.\n\nThat’s not just me being sensitive. I’m careful in my work. I know how these files are supposed to behave. And when the same kind of thing keeps happening in a way that puts the blame on me, it’s hard for me to accept that it’s just my perception distorting it.\n\nAnd yes, IT keeps saying the audit trail is clean, but that doesn’t really settle the matter for me. A clean audit trail doesn’t mean nothing happened. It just means it was arranged in a way that doesn’t show up as obvious tampering. That’s exactly what worries me — that the record has been made to look normal while still directing responsibility back toward me.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m exaggerating it. I think other people are minimizing it because the changes are small and technical, but that’s partly why it works.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your personal relationships with family or friends since you started suspecting sabotage at work?\n\nPatient: Not really in a broad sense, no. I mean, I wouldn’t say my whole life has fallen apart around this. With my family, I still do the usual things. I pay the bills, I keep up with household routines, I’m present for the normal family things as much as anyone with work stress is. I still do my translation work, I still manage the team’s workflow, I still get through the practical responsibilities.\n\nWhere it changes is when the work issue comes up. If family or friends ask how work is, or if I start explaining the file history, the timestamps, certain coworkers, that’s when it gets tense or I can feel people wanting to move away from the subject. And I probably do go into too much detail, because I’m trying to make them understand that it’s not just “office stress.” But outside of that specific topic, I’m functioning normally.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say my relationships have changed in some general way. It’s more like there’s this one area that causes friction. If nobody brings up the project files or the people involved at work, things are basically the same. But if that comes up, then yes, I can get stuck on it, and I think people around me don’t always know what to do with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is sabotaging you if everyone at work, including your supervisors and IT, told you that your concerns are unfounded and that there's no evidence of sabotage?\n\nPatient: I think… if everyone said that, I would hear it, but I don’t think it would change what I’ve seen.\n\nThat’s probably not the answer you’re hoping for, but I’m trying to be honest. If my supervisor, IT, everyone said, “There’s no evidence,” I would understand that they’re saying the official record doesn’t show anything. But the official record is exactly the thing I’m worried about. The whole point is that the changes are being made in a way that looks legitimate afterward.\n\nFor months I’ve been seeing the same pattern in the project files. It’s not one isolated mistake. It’s these tiny changes in the translation memory, little timestamp shifts, certain subcontractors’ names appearing around the same kinds of edits, and one colleague in particular seeming to be connected to those moments. Then later, when someone reviews it, the record makes it look like my quality control failed, like I missed something I should have caught.\n\nSo if IT says the audit trail is clean, I don’t take that as proof that nothing happened. I take it as proof that nothing obvious was left behind. Those are different things. A clean audit trail doesn’t reassure me when the outcome keeps being that responsibility points back to me.\n\nI know that may sound stubborn. I know people might say, “If everyone disagrees with you, doesn’t that mean you should reconsider?” And I do think about that. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. But I’m the one who has been going through the files and seeing how these details line up. So yes, even if everyone at work said my concerns were unfounded, I think I would still believe someone has arranged the records to put the blame on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's anything that could change your belief about being sabotaged at work, no matter what evidence or explanations you might be given?\n\nPatient: I suppose in theory there could be something, yes. I’m not saying I’m incapable of hearing evidence. If someone could actually show me a complete, independent reconstruction of what happened in the project files — not just “the audit trail is clean,” but something that explains why those translation memory changes happened, why the timestamps shifted the way they did, why the same subcontractors’ names keep appearing around those edits, and why that one colleague is repeatedly near the sequence — then I would have to look at that.\n\nBut honestly, just being told “there’s no evidence” doesn’t change it for me. That’s the part people don’t seem to understand. The concern is not that someone made some obvious crude alteration and forgot to cover their tracks. The concern is that the record has been arranged so that afterward it looks normal, and it looks like my quality control failed. So when IT says the audit trail is clean, that doesn’t answer the real question for me. It only tells me nothing obvious was left behind.\n\nSo… could something change my mind? Maybe, if it actually accounted for the pattern I’ve been seeing for months in a way that made sense. But I haven’t been given that. I’ve been given reassurance, and I’ve been given explanations that feel too general. They don’t explain why these same small details keep lining up in a way that puts the blame back on me.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m setting the bar very high. Maybe I am. But if someone is arranging the records carefully, then ordinary reassurance isn’t enough. I would need something that actually explains the pattern, not just dismisses it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think any amount of evidence or explanation could ever make you doubt that someone is sabotaging you at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… “any amount” is a hard way to put it. I don’t want to sound like I’m saying nothing could ever make me doubt myself, because that sounds unreasonable, and I don’t think I’m an unreasonable person. But at the same time, what I’ve seen in the files isn’t just a feeling I woke up with one morning.\n\nI’m here because for months there’s been this same pattern in the project files. Certain subcontractors’ names keep showing up around these tiny changes to the translation memory, and the timestamps shift in ways that don’t make sense if it’s just normal workflow. And there’s one colleague in particular who keeps being close to these sequences of edits. Then later, when the work is reviewed, it ends up looking like my quality control failed, like I missed things I should have caught.\n\nSo if someone just gives me more explanations like, “IT checked and the audit trail is clean,” that doesn’t really make me doubt it. Because a clean audit trail is exactly what I’d expect if the record had been arranged carefully. It doesn’t answer why the same small details keep lining up in a way that puts the blame back on me.\n\nCould some kind of evidence make me question it? Maybe, if it actually explained the whole pattern and didn’t just wave it away. But honestly, with what I’ve seen over these months, I don’t think ordinary evidence or reassurance would be enough. It would have to show me, in detail, why those changes happened and why they keep pointing back at me without someone having set it up that way. Until then, no, I don’t think I’d really be able to doubt that someone is sabotaging me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is sabotaging you if you were shown definitive proof that all the changes to the project files were made accidentally or as part of a normal workflow?\n\nPatient: If it were truly definitive proof… I mean, I know that’s what you’re asking. If someone could show me, beyond any doubt, that every single change was accidental or just normal workflow, then I suppose I should say yes, that would change my mind.\n\nBut I have trouble with that word “definitive,” because from where I’m sitting, the record itself is part of the problem. For months I’ve been seeing the same pattern in the project files — the same kinds of tiny changes in the translation memory, the timestamp shifts, certain subcontractors’ names appearing around those edits, and one colleague in particular always seeming to be near that sequence. And then the end result is that it looks like my quality control failed.\n\nSo if someone showed me a report saying, “These were all normal workflow,” I wouldn’t automatically accept that as definitive. I’d be thinking, okay, but who produced that report, what data did they rely on, and is it just repeating the same audit trail that’s already been cleaned up? Because IT saying the audit trail is clean doesn’t settle it for me. A clean audit trail can just mean the record was arranged carefully enough that nothing obvious was left behind.\n\nI’m not trying to be difficult. I understand that if something really proved it was all accidental, then I’d have to take that seriously. But honestly, with what I’ve been seeing, I don’t know what would count as proof unless it explained the whole pattern in detail — why those same people keep showing up, why the timestamps shift that way, why the translation memory changes keep ending up making me look responsible. Otherwise I’d still think the records had been arranged to put the blame on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I understand why you’re asking that, because motive matters. I’ve asked myself that too — why would someone bother, why me, why go to that much trouble over project files and translation memory entries? I don’t have a neat answer for that.

But the thing is, the absence of a clear motive doesn’t erase the pattern I’ve been seeing. For months, it’s been the same kind of thing: tiny changes around the translation memory, timestamps that don’t sit right with the workflow, certain subcontractors’ names appearing near those edits, and one colleague in particular repeatedly close to the sequence. Then when it all gets reviewed afterward, it somehow lands on me — like my quality control failed, like I missed things I wouldn’t normally miss.

So if you’re asking, “Would I stop believing it just because I can’t explain exactly why they’d want to do it?” No, I don’t think I would. I’d wonder about the reason, yes. It bothers me that I can’t fully explain it. But I still know what I’m seeing in the files.

And when IT says the audit trail is clean, that doesn’t settle it for me, because a clean record can just mean it was arranged carefully. That’s part of what worries me. If someone is making it look normal afterward, then of course the official explanation is going to be, “Nothing happened.”

So no, the lack of a logical motive wouldn’t be enough for me to dismiss it. It might make me question the reason behind it, but not the fact that the record has been arranged in a way that puts the blame on me.

Then later, when the work is reviewed, it ends up looking like my quality control failed, like I missed things I should have caught.\n\nSo if someone just gives me more explanations like, “IT checked and the audit trail is clean,” that doesn’t really make me doubt it. Because a clean audit trail is exactly what I’d expect if the record had been arranged carefully. It doesn’t answer why the same small details keep lining up in a way that puts the blame back on me.\n\nCould some kind of evidence make me question it? Maybe, if it actually explained the whole pattern and didn’t just wave it away. But honestly, with what I’ve seen over these months, I don’t think ordinary evidence or reassurance would be enough. It would have to show me, in detail, why those changes happened and why they keep pointing back at me without someone having set it up that way. Until then, no, I don’t think I’d really be able to doubt that someone is sabotaging me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is sabotaging you if you were shown definitive proof that all the changes to the project files were made accidentally or as part of a normal workflow?\n\nPatient: If it were truly definitive proof… I mean, I know that’s what you’re asking. If someone could show me, beyond any doubt, that every single change was accidental or just normal workflow, then I suppose I should say yes, that would change my mind.\n\nBut I have trouble with that word “definitive,” because from where I’m sitting, the record itself is part of the problem. For months I’ve been seeing the same pattern in the project files — the same kinds of tiny changes in the translation memory, the timestamp shifts, certain subcontractors’ names appearing around those edits, and one colleague in particular always seeming to be near that sequence. And then the end result is that it looks like my quality control failed.\n\nSo if someone showed me a report saying, “These were all normal workflow,” I wouldn’t automatically accept that as definitive. I’d be thinking, okay, but who produced that report, what data did they rely on, and is it just repeating the same audit trail that’s already been cleaned up? Because IT saying the audit trail is clean doesn’t settle it for me. A clean audit trail can just mean the record was arranged carefully enough that nothing obvious was left behind.\n\nI’m not trying to be difficult. I understand that if something really proved it was all accidental, then I’d have to take that seriously. But honestly, with what I’ve been seeing, I don’t know what would count as proof unless it explained the whole pattern in detail — why those same people keep showing up, why the timestamps shift that way, why the translation memory changes keep ending up making me look responsible. Otherwise I’d still think the records had been arranged to put the blame on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still believe someone is sabotaging you at work even if there was no logical explanation for why they would want to do so?\n\nPatient: I understand why you’re asking that, because motive matters. I’ve asked myself that too — why would someone bother, why me, why go to that much trouble over project files and translation memory entries? I don’t have a neat answer for that.\n\nBut the thing is, the absence of a clear motive doesn’t erase the pattern I’ve been seeing. For months, it’s been the same kind of thing: tiny changes around the translation memory, timestamps that don’t sit right with the workflow, certain subcontractors’ names appearing near those edits, and one colleague in particular repeatedly close to the sequence. Then when it all gets reviewed afterward, it somehow lands on me — like my quality control failed, like I missed things I wouldn’t normally miss.\n\nSo if you’re asking, “Would I stop believing it just because I can’t explain exactly why they’d want to do it?” No, I don’t think I would. I’d wonder about the reason, yes. It bothers me that I can’t fully explain it. But I still know what I’m seeing in the files.\n\nAnd when IT says the audit trail is clean, that doesn’t settle it for me, because a clean record can just mean it was arranged carefully. That’s part of what worries me. If someone is making it look normal afterward, then of course the official explanation is going to be, “Nothing happened.”\n\nSo no, the lack of a logical motive wouldn’t be enough for me to dismiss it. It might make me question the reason behind it, but not the fact that the record has been arranged in a way that puts the blame on me."
  }
